## Page 1


MIXING TIMES FOR GLAUBER DYNAMICS OF LOZENGE TILINGS OF
THE HEXAGON
AMOL AGGARWAL AND FABIO TONINELLI
Abstract. We prove that the continuous-time, single-flip Glauber dynamics for lozenge tilings of
the size-N hexagon mix in time N2+o(1). This was predicted to hold on fairly general domains of
diameter N (on the basis of the “Lifshitz law” heuristic) but had previously only been established
in domains such that the associated limit shape has no frozen facets. To access the hexagon, we
introduce a multi-scale comparison argument between the height function of the random tiling
evolving under the Glauber dynamics and the limit shape of a volume-tilted tiling (whose tilting
parameter varies suitably in time).

1. Introduction
1.1. The lozenge tiling Glauber dynamic on the hexagon. Random lozenge tilings, or equiv-
alently the dimer model on the honeycomb lattice, are a classical subject of study in statistical me-
chanics, probability and combinatorics; see e.g., the monograph [Gor21]. To introduce the problem,
let TN, N ∈N, be the infinite triangular grid with equilateral triangular faces of side-length 1/N,
and let D be a bounded domain of the plane that can be tiled by lozenge-shaped tiles, obtained as
the union of two adjacent triangular faces. Call ΩN the set of all lozenge tilings of D and PN the
uniform probability measure on ΩN.
In addition to being of intrinsic interest, random tilings are archetypes of strongly correlated
systems that are very sensitive to boundary conditions. They may be viewed as models of random
surfaces, which are known [CLP98, CKP01] to converge to a limit shape as N tends to ∞. A
1

AMOL AGGARWAL AND FABIO TONINELLI
salient feature of these limit shapes is that they are highly inhomogeneous. They admit frozen
regions, where the surface is almost deterministically flat, and liquid regions, where it appears more
rough and random; the curve separating these phases is called an arctic boundary [JPS98]. Across
these different regions, the tiling admits considerably different asymptotic behaviors, which can
be computed exactly due to the underlying determinantal structure of the model [Kas61, Ken97]
that becomes explicit on specific polygonal domains, such as the hexagon [Joh02]. For example,
when D is a regular hexagon, in the liquid region the tiling has Gaussian free field fluctuations
[Pet15]; around the arctic boundary it has Kardar–Parisi–Zhang (KPZ) fluctuations [OR03, FS03,
BKMM07, Pet14]; and near points where the arctic boundary meets the hexagon, called tangency
locations, the tiling has Gaussian unitary ensemble (GUE) fluctuations [JN06, OR06].
While the measure PN is by now well studied, local Markovian dynamics on ΩN (admitting PN
as stationary measure) and their mixing properties are still poorly understood. In this work, we
consider the most natural such dynamic, that is the single-flip Glauber dynamic on ΩN, which is
the continuous-time Markov chain on ΩN whose updates are the elementary rotations depicted in
Figure 1 (right), both having transition rate 1. This dynamic is routinely used1, to numerically
sample uniformly chosen random lozenge tilings of the domain D (and can be implemented within
the coupling-from-the-past framework; see [PW96] and [LP17, Chapter 25]).
We mention also
that the single-flip Glauber dynamic is the zero-temperature limit of the Glauber dynamic of the
three-dimensional Ising model with Dobrushin boundary conditions [CMST11, CMT12].
The uniform measure PN on ΩN is reversible for the Glauber dynamic and is its unique stationary
measure. Since the state space is finite, the total variation mixing time tN
mix is also finite. The
question of quantifying tN
mix as a function of N has an interesting history, and an incomplete status.
It was first proven in [LRS01] that a non-local version of the Glauber dynamic, called the “tower-
move dynamic,” mixes in polynomial (in N) time. Via standard spectral gap comparison tools,
this implies polynomial time mixing for the single-flip dynamic, as well. Subsequently, when D is
the regular hexagon X of side-length 1 with sides parallel to the axis of the triangular grid (that
can be tiled by exactly 3N 2 tiles), the mixing time of the tower-move dynamic was determined in
[Wil04] to be of order N 2 log N (with upper and lower bounds differing by a constant prefactor).
This result implies (via the above spectral gap comparison) that tN
mix ≲N 6+o(1) [RT00]. A more
refined comparison argument (that combines the analysis of the tower-dynamic mixing time with the
censoring inequality [PW13]) allows to prove tN
mix ≲N 4+o(1) [CMST11]; this was the best existing
upper bound, for the mixing time of the Glauber dynamic in the hexagon, before the present work.
However, on the basis of a general “mean curvature motion heuristic” (see also Section 1.2 for
more discussion), it was predicted that tN
mix ≈N 2 also for the single-flip dynamic, possibly with
logarithmic sub-leading corrections. This conjecture turned out to be rather elusive, for several
reasons. First, in contrast with the tower-move dynamic, coupling arguments fail to provide any
contraction for the Glauber dynamic.
Second, the dynamic has no perturbative parameter to
tune (such as the temperature for Ising models, or the number of colors and graph degree for
random colorings). Third, equilibrium correlations for the system are not summable, so one cannot
exploit strong spatial mixing properties (in the spirit of [Mar99]) to upper bound the mixing time.
Moreover, due to the hard constraints intrinsic in the tiling model, configurations can be blocked
(unable to perform any local move) in arbitrarily large regions of the graph.
Still, a first step towards the conjecture was done in [CMT12], where the bounds
N 2 ≲tN
mix ≲N 2+o(1)

were proven for the single-flip dynamic in a tilable domain D of the plane (of diameter of order 1),
provided that the limit shape on D is affine. Later, (1.1) was generalized [LT15, LT23] to the case
where the limit shape is C∞. These partial results point out that the geometry of the domain D,
and in particular the regularity properties of the associated limit shape, play a subtle role in the
convergence to equilibrium.
Arguably, the most natural and classical geometric domain where lozenge tilings can be studied
is the hexagon X, and neither of the cited results [CMT12, LT15, LT23] applies there. Indeed,
the limit shape on X [CLP98] (as well as on other polygonal domains [KO07, ADPZ26]) admits an
arctic boundary, along which the limit shape is not smooth.
The main result of the present work is a proof of the asymptotic tN
mix ≈N 2 for the Glauber
dynamic on X, up to sub-leading multiplicative corrections:
Theorem 1.1. For any δ > 0, there exist constants κ > 0 and C = C(δ) > 0 such that the mixing
time of the single-flip Glauber dynamic on tilings of the hexagon X satisfies
κN 2 ≤tN
mix ≤CN 2+δ.
(1.2)
With minor modifications, one can also prove the same statement for a hexagon of fixed positive
side-lengths a, b, c (in which case the constants κ, C would depend on a, b, c). However, we will not
pursue this here, in order to avoid making the notation heavier.
The effort in proving Theorem 1.1 is the upper bound: the argument for tN
mix ≥κN 2, already
given in [CMT12, Section 5.2] for the dynamic in a domain Ω⊂TN where the limit shape is affine,
works identically for the dynamic in X and we will not repeat it.
1.2. Proof ideas. The conjecture tmix = N 2+o(1) is based on the “Lifshitz law” heuristic [Lif62]
stating that, upon rescaling time by N 2 and in the limit N →∞, the limiting evolution of the
tiling height function φ under the Glauber dynamics should follow an anisotropic variant of mean
curvature motion, of the form
∂tφ = µ(∇φ) · Lφ.
(1.3)
In (1.3), L is an explicit semi-elliptic, non-linear operator (related to the surface tension functional
of random tilings) and µ(∇φ) is a certain slope-dependent “mobility coefficient” governing the
speed of the evolution; see [Spo93] for further commentary. The limit (1.3) has only been proven
in the case of the tower-move dynamics [LT18] (assuming the solution to (1.3) is C2), where the
associated mobility coefficient µ is known explicitly. For the Glauber dynamics, there is no predicted
expression for µ. Moreover, under hexagonal boundary conditions, the solution φ to (1.3) should
not be C2, so even the well-posedness of (1.3) is unclear.
At least the first issue was circumvented in [CMT12, LT15, LT23], by instead proving that the
evolution Ht of the tiling height function under the Glauber dynamics likely remains between two
deterministic “barrier” functions φ−
t and φ+
t . The latter might be viewed as “proxies” to solutions
of (1.3) and were obtained by perturbing H by functions ψ±
t whose Hessian dominates that of H.
The latter in particular necessitates that H is C2, which does not hold if H is the limit shape for a
random tiling of the hexagon.
To address this, we instead introduce different barriers, given by the limit shapes Hq of a random
tiling of the hexagon, weighted by eq vol, where vol denotes the volume of the stepped surface
associated with the tiling. Such “q-deformed” tiling models have been studied quite extensively
over the past two decades [CK01, OR03, KO07, BGR10, GH24]. Here, we use their limit shapes to

study the Glauber dynamics of the original (q = 0) tiling model. In particular, we prove (Proposition
5.14) by induction on t that
H−q(t) ≤Ht ≤Hq(t),
(1.4)
holds with high probability, where q(t) > 0 depends suitably on t (and N), decreasing to 0 as t
becomes large. We will eventually take q(t) as small as N −1 for t = N 2+o(1). This implies at such
times that the height Ht is so close to the limit shape H associated to the uniform measure PN,
that after another time-lag of N 2+o(1) equilibrium is reached.
These Hq limit shapes are both fully explicit [BGR10, GH24] and more closely model the behavior
of the Glauber dynamics on the hexagon than the barrier functions φ±
t used in earlier works.2 For
instance, they also admit arctic boundaries separating liquid from frozen phases. We will see that
the mechanism under which the Glauber dynamics locally mixes will vary in mesoscopic regions
between the bulk of the liquid phase and the arctic boundary, and that this must be taken into
account when establishing (1.4).
To implement this idea, we begin by showing a “base case” for (1.4), verifying it (Theorem 4.3)
when t = N 2+o(1) and q(t) = q0 > 0 is a constant that is arbitrarily small but independent of
N. First, we perturb the boundary conditions of H to make the associated limit shape smooth
(Proposition 4.6), enabling us to apply the prior results [LT15, LT23] to show that the Glauber
dynamics under these perturbed boundary conditions mix in time N2+o(1). By comparing these to
the original Glauber dynamics, we obtain (Proposition 4.5) that |Ht −H| ≲q0 for t = N 2+o(1) and
fixed q0 > 0. This is in the direction of (1.4) but does not quite verify it where Hq0 is frozen, as
there we have H−q0 = Hq0. These regions are addressed by induction on N, involving “embedding”
smaller hexagons (whose frozen regions contain those of Hq0) into the original one; see Section 4.2.2
for further details.
Next, we must show (1.4) for q(t) < q0, which is the main task in this work. A primary heuristic
here is that, throughout the Glauber dynamics, one should expect all level lines of Ht to move at
a speed of order N −2. However, unlike on domains admitting C2 limit shapes, different level lines
of Ht on the hexagon should exhibit different fluctuations. Thus, it should take different amounts
of time to “detect” whether the level line has moved; in this way, local mixing occurs at different
rates across the hexagon. For example, in the bulk of the liquid region, the level lines should have
N o(1)−1 fluctuations, so their motion is detected after time N 1+o(1); generically along the arctic
boundary, they should have No(1)−2/3 fluctuations, so their motion is detected after time N 4/3+o(1);
and at the tangency locations, they should have N o(1)−1/2 fluctuations, so their motion is detected
only after time N 3/2+o(1).
To resolve this, we decompose the hexagon X into “annuli” (Definition 5.1), in which these level
line fluctuations (and thus the rate of local mixing) are of the same order. We then establish the
following two types of statements; in what follows, we focus on decreasing the upper bound on
Ht (increasing the lower bound is entirely analogous). The first, which we may refer to as “liquid
phase mixing,” states that if the height function Ht is upper bounded in an annulus not too close to
the arctic boundary, then the upper bound on Ht improves (that is, it decreases further) on inner
annuli, after running the Glauber dynamics for a certain time (Lemma 5.6). The second, which we
may refer to as “edge phase mixing,” states that if Ht is upper bounded in an annulus reasonably
close to the arctic boundary, then this bound extends to the outer annuli (up to a negligible error)
2In fact, if one stops these Glauber dynamics before they mix, the associated height function looks numerically
(see, e.g., https://lpetrov.cc/lozenge-draw/) qualitatively quite similar to (though is not predicted to exactly coincide
with) some Hq.

5
after some time (Lemmas 5.7 and 5.8). By combining these two statements, we then prove (1.4) by
induction on t.
To show these annulus mixing results, we first reduce them to their “local versions” (Propositions
6.4, 7.6, and 7.7) that restrict to mesoscopic cells contained within the annuli. The benefit to doing
this is that the limit shapes Hq are more regular in these mesoscopic cells. For example, although
Hq is not C2 near the arctic boundary, it becomes smooth upon an explicit rescaling3 dependent
on the cell’s location (Proposition 3.27). Given this, the proof of local bulk mixing (Proposition
6.4) uses an idea introduced in [LT15]. Specifically, we compare (the “regular rescaling” of) the
limit shape Hq(t) to that Ha,b,c of an unweighted (that is, uniform) tiling on a certain a × b × c
hexagon, so that their gradients coincide at the cell’s center but Hq is “more concave” than Ha,b,c
(Proposition 3.28). Using the added concavity of Hq, with concentration bounds for the unweighted
tiling around Ha,b,c, we show that Ht′ must “deflate” to a smaller Hq(t′) after some time t′ −t; see
Section 6.3 for further details.
To show the local edge mixing results, we separate into cases, depending on whether the cell is
away from or at the arctic boundary (Propositions 7.6 or 7.7), and whether it is away from or at
the tangency location (Sections 8.1 or 8.2, and Sections 9.1 or 9.2). The proofs in all of these cases
are quite similar to each other, but they differ from the bulk case, since the limit shape Hq will not
behave regularly in our mesoscopic cells close to the arctic boundary. However, the level lines of
Hq will be more regular (Proposition 3.26). So, to show the local edge mixing results, we develop
an analog of the local bulk mixing reasoning that compares the convexity properties between the
level lines of the tiling height functions (which are equivalent to non-intersecting random walks)
approximating Hq and Ha,b,c. See the beginning of Section 8.1.2 for a more detailed exposition.
In order to implement the above strategy, we need to prove, among others, several analytic
statements concerning the volume-tilted limit shape Hq, that make precise the intuition that Hq
becomes more concave as q grows, and that the curvature of its level lines changes in a specific way
as a function of q. These statements are given in Section 3 and proved (by direct, though tedious,
Taylor expansions of the exact expressions for these limit shapes) in Appendices B and C.
1.3. Relation with existing literature. The Lifshitz law heuristic mentioned above applies to
any reasonable reversible interface dynamics in (d + 1) dimensions, where d refers to the space
dimension and 1 to time, corresponding in the case of lozenge tilings to d = 2. This heuristic leads
to the conjecture that the inverse spectral gap is of order N 2 and that the mixing time is of order
N 2 times a logarithmic (in N) factor, the latter originating from the fact that once the interface is
macroscopically close to the equilibrium one (which happens after a time of the order of the inverse
spectral gap), it still takes some time before its law is close to the equilibrium one in total variation
distance.
The prior rigorous proofs of the tmix ≍N 2 log N asymptotic for interface dynamics had been
been mostly limited to (1+1)-dimensional random interface models [CLL22, MS12, Lac16] (see also
[Fun16] and references therein). A simplifying feature of one-dimensional interface models is that
their stationary measure, when viewed in terms of the interface gradients, has an i.i.d. structure. In
contrast, for random tilings, equilibrium correlations have a slow decay in space, of the order of the
squared inverse distance. Another case where such results were proven is the Glauber dynamic of
the (d + 1)-dimensional Gaussian lattice free field [GG23] (where the spectral gap can be computed
exactly). In a different but related direction, [BB20] proved sharp results for the inverse spectral gap
of Glauber dynamics of interface models on hierarchical lattices; the inverse gap has the same order
3This is sometimes called a “blow-up” procedure in the literature on obstacle problems [Caf98].


## Page 6

AMOL AGGARWAL AND FABIO TONINELLI
as that of the corresponding Gaussian free field dynamic. We point out that [CLL22, GG23, Lac16]
further prove that these dynamics exhibit the cutoff (in total variation) phenomenon.
Finally, let us mention the earlier works [CMT11, GPR09] on mixing of Glauber dynamics of
random lozenge tilings, that take a point of view quite different from that of the present paper.
These works study a biased version of the Glauber dynamic, where the two updates in Figure 1
have different transition rates. In this case, the phenomenology (both dynamic and equilibrium)
is very different. First, the stationary measure of the biased dynamic is not uniform on the set of
all tilings, but is instead the volume-tilted measure eq vol, where q = −γN is of order N, and the
parameter γ ̸= 0 is related to the ratio of the two transition rates. Such a measure concentrates on
configurations whose volume is of order 1/N, while typical configurations sampled from PN have
volume of order N 2. Also, the Lifshitz law heuristic does not apply to the biased dynamic, because
the drift induced by the bias dominates the curvature effect; in fact, the mixing time and inverse
spectral gap turn out to be of order N and O(1) respectively, in contrast with N2+o(1) and N 2 for
the symmetric dynamic we study here.

2.2. The Glauber dynamics on lozenge tilings.
2.2.1. Definition of the dynamics and general facts. In this section we define the Glauber dynamic
on lozenge tilings of a finite, simply-connected, tilable domain D ⊂R2. First we introduce some
notation. Given η ∈ΩD, we let Mη be the set of points z ∈D ∩TN such that z belongs to three
tiles in η of the three possible orientations (see Figure 1(b)). Given z ∈Mη, we let η(z) be the tiling
obtained by rotating the three tiles that contain z by an angle π around z (see Figure 1 (b)).
Definition 2.8 (Glauber dynamics). The lozenge tiling Glauber dynamic on D is the continuous-
time Markov process with state space ΩD defined by the properties that the only allowed transitions
from a configuration η ∈ΩD are the configurations {η(z)}z∈Mη and that each such transition occurs
according to an exponential clock of rate 1.
Since the transitions η →η(z) and η(z) →η have the same rate, the uniform measure on ΩD
is stationary and reversible. It is quickly verified that, when D = Xa,b,c, the Glauber dynamic is
irreducible.

Definition 2.10 (Constrained dynamic). Fix H−, H+ : D 7→R with H−≤H+ pointwise, let ΩH±

H+ is the Markov chain on ΩH± that is defined just like the original Glauber dynamic, except that
any update that would violate the constraint H−≤Hηt ≤H+ is discarded.

2.2.2. The Peres-Winkler censoring inequality. The Peres-Winkler censoring inequality [PW13]
states that, for certain monotone Markov dynamics under certain initial data, censoring (that is,
ignoring) updates slows down convergence to equilibrium. We give in Proposition 2.17 the precise
statement we need in the specific case of the lozenge tiling dynamic.
Definition 2.15 (Restricted dynamic). Given the Glauber dynamic in a tilable domain D and a
(not necessarily tilable) subset D′ ⊂D, the Glauber dynamic restricted to D′ is the one obtained
by ignoring the Poisson clocks in D \ D′.
Definition 2.16. Given H+, H−: Xa,b,c 7→R, with H−≤H+ pointwise, let P+[H−, H+] denote

The function ϕ is called limit shape (in the domain D, with boundary condition f) and it satisfies
the following monotonicity property and maximum principle:
Proposition 3.6. Let D be an open bounded subset of R2 with piecewise smooth boundary, let
f1, f2 : ∂D 7→R be such that Adm(D; f1), Adm(D; f2) ̸= ∅and let ϕ1, ϕ2 be the associated limit
shapes. Then, it holds:
• If f1(z) ≤f2(z) for all z ∈∂D, then ϕ1(z) ≤ϕ2(z) for all z ∈D;
• The maximum of ϕ1 −ϕ2 in ¯D is realized on ∂D.
Proof. The first statement is proven for instance in [Agg23, Lemma 2.5]. The second immediately
follows from the first, with the fact that ∂D is compact and that, if two boundary heights differ by
an additive constant, the limit shapes do as well.
□
Several facts about the limit shape Hq;a,b,c and arctic boundary of the volume-tilted measure
are proven in Section B. We begin with the following lemma about the arctic boundary Aq;a,b,c.
This statement is known, but we are not aware of a proof written anywhere, so we provide one in
Section B.2 below.
Lemma 3.7. The arctic boundary Aq;a,b,c is a smooth curve that is tangent to each edge of Xa,b,c
at exactly one point.
In view of Lemma 3.7, we pose the following definition.

For q = 0, the arctic curve Aa,b,c is the ellipse inscribed in Xa,b,c [CKP01]. It is therefore natural
to expect that Aq;a,b,c is convex for q small. This is confirmed by the next result:
Lemma 3.10. There exists a constant ε > 0 such that, for any q ∈(−ε, ε) and a, b, c ∈(1−ε, 1+ε),
the arctic boundary Aq;a,b,c is a smooth, convex curve, whose curvature is bounded below by a positive
constant κ = κ(a, b, c) > 0.

3.2. Properties of the limit shape and of its level lines. This section collects several results
about the behavior of Hq;a,b,c and of its level lines, defined below, near the arctic boundary. As
mentioned in Section 3, these limit shapes admit an explicit, though slightly intricate, formula in
terms of elementary functions (provided in Appendix B.1). As such, the proofs of the limit shape
results in this section will amount to a fairly direct (but tedious) Taylor expansion of those formulas.

recall the notation xSW

The next proposition (shown in Section C.3) will be used to compare the level lines of Hq and
of Ha,b,c. Equation (3.19) indicates the level lines of the q-deformed limit shape are “more convex”
than those of the non-deformed limit shape, upon approximately matching (after a shift in the
hexagon, given by r and r′ in the x and y coordinates, respectively) their gradients through (3.17)
and (3.18); (3.21) allows a specific inequality between these derivatives. See Figure 6. A heuristic
explanation for this increased convexity is as follows. Suppose one fixes the values of a level line
at two x-coordinates, x−and x+. Then increasing q would, in order to maximize the volume of
the associated height function, cause this level line to extend further to the right in the interval
(x−, x+), thereby increasing its convexity.
Simultaneously enabling an approximate match between these gradients but a gain in convexity
when comparing these limit shapes can require a slight change in the dimensions of the hexagon,
from 1 × 1 × 1 for the q-deformed limit shape to some a × b × c for the non-deformed one. This
gain (3.19) in convexity is not imposed too close to the left edge of the arctic boundary, where we
instead only require that the level lines of Hq are above those of Ha,b,c, by (3.20). The estimate
(3.23) lower bounds the height function Ha,b,c in terms of Hq (observe that this corresponds to
upper bounding the level lines Ua,b,c of Ha,b,c in terms of the Uq, which the estimates (3.19) and
(3.20) do for the h0-level line).

The rest of the proof is organized as follows. In Section 4.2 we will prove Theorem 4.3. In
Section 4.3 we show how this implies Theorem 4.1. Finally, the proof of Theorem 4.4 is contained
in Sections 5.
4.2. Proof of Theorem 4.3.
4.2.1. Perturbing the boundary condition. The first step is to prove that, for any δ, ω > 0, after
time of order N 2+δ, the height function is w.o.p. within ω from the macroscopic height. If the
limit shape had no frozen regions, this would follow from the main result in [LT15]. In our case
this assumption fails, but the problem can be resolved by a suitable perturbation of the boundary
height. Let f : ∂X 7→R denote the restriction to ∂X of the height function of any lozenge tiling of
X. Note that f is affine along each side of the hexagon.
Proposition 4.5. Assume that there exists a sequence of boundary height functions f (n) : ∂X →R,
for each n ≥1, such that Adm(X; f (n)) is not empty (recall Definition 3.3) and

that ∇H(n) ∈T and dist(∇H(n), ∂T ) ≥1/(2n) hold almost everywhere in X, where T was
defined in (3.3).
Then, for the Glauber dynamic in X, for every δ, L, ω > 0 there exists C < ∞such that, for every
N,

note that both u(·) := H(n)(·) and v(·) := H(n)(· + (0, r)) are limit shapes of the tiling model in
X and X −(0, r), respectively. In particular, they are both minimizers in X ∩(X −(0, r)) (with
different boundary data). Let (x, y), (x, y + r) ∈X, so that (x, y) ∈X ∩(X −(0, r)). Then,

enough (but independently of N). Also, δ ∈(0, 10−1000) is fixed throughout to be a sufficiently

large constants that are much larger one with respect to the other (and A), in a certain order. The

impose that they satisfy

5.2. Height reduction across annuli. In this section, we state three variants of the following
statement. If a height function H0 initially satisfies w.o.p. an upper bound of the type H0 ≤Hq
(possibly with a small additive error) in a certain region R ⊆X, then after a suitable amount of
time under the Glauber dynamics, Ht satisfies w.o.p. the improved upper bound Ht ≤Hq′, with
q′ < q, in a modified region R′ ⊆X. Lemma 5.6 addresses regions in the bulk liquid phase, while
Lemma 5.7 and Lemma 5.8 address regions in the edge phase (close to or at the arctic boundary,
respectively).
In all cases, the time t at which this improved bound holds will be at least N 2(q −q′) (and
will attain this bound, up to powers of Nδ, if q −q′ is made as large as permitted). This suggests
that the parameter q defining the upper barrier Hq decreases “at speed” N −2−o(1). As such, q
should reduce from ε0 to 0 in time N 2+o(1), which is consistent with the notion that the Glauber
dynamics mix in time N 2+o(1). Furthermore, in all of these statements, we must run for time7 2kN
to “witness” a height reduction on the annulus Ak. To heuristically see why this should be the case,
observe that one expects q to change by 2kN −1 after time 2kN. Hence, Lemma 3.21 indicates that
the height function (Ht) on Aq′
k reduces by (dzez;q′)1/22kN −1 in this time. Since (dzez;q′)1/2 ∼2−k
on Aq′
k , this reduction is around N o(1)−1, which is the minimal that can be “detected” (as it is the
minimal height difference).
The following lemma, to be shown in Section 6.1, states the bulk liquid phase mixing bound. It
indicates that, if a height function H0 is initially bounded by some Hq, then running the Glauber
dynamics (Ht)t≥0 decreases this upper bound to Hq′ at points z in the bulk liquid phase (see the
bound k ≤ℓ0(q) + 5 in (5.10)), for some q′ < q.

5.3. Proof of Theorem 4.4. To show Theorem 4.4, we will use the results from Section 5.2 in an
inductive way, to successively improve the upper bound on the height function under the Glauber
dynamics (in suitably chosen time intervals), to reduce it from the initial Hq0 to Hqimax , with qimax
so small that Hqimax is barely distinguishable from the equilibrium height. The three results from
that section that we will use are Lemma 5.6, which applies in the bulk liquid phase; Lemma 5.7,
which applies in the edge phase not at the arctic boundary; and Lemma 5.8, which applies at the
arctic boundary.
All of these decrease the value of q in the function Hq upper bounding the Glauber dynamics. It
will be beneficial to decrease q by as much as possible under each application of the above results;
this is limited by Lemma 5.6. Ignoring factors of N δ, after taking k = ℓ0(q) + 5 to the maximum

6.3. Proof of Proposition 6.4. In this section we show Proposition 6.4, whose proof will be
similar to that of [LT15, Claim 6.3], upon replacing the use of [LT15, Theorem 4.4] in the proof
of [LT15, Proposition 6.7] with Proposition 3.28 here. Specifically (omitting factors of Nδ in this
informal discussion), we will first use Proposition 3.28 to match at z0 the values of Hq and its
gradient with those of some q = 0 limit shape Ha,b,c, so that the latter is “less concave” than Hq
at z0. This difference in concavity will ensure that Ha,b,c −Hq ≫N −1 on the boundary ∂Y of the
cell containing z0. Hence, monotonicity will imply that the stationary measure for (Ht) on Y, with
boundary data Hq on ∂Y, is lower than Ha,b,c by at least N −1. Since Ha,b,c and Hq are shifted
to match at z0, this will imply that Ht decreases by at least N −1 at z0. By Lemma 3.21, this
corresponds (as in (6.10)) to decreasing q by N −1(dz0ez0;q)−1/2, in agreement with (6.11). We next
implement this in detail.

7. Height reduction in the edge phase
7.1. Proof of Lemma 5.7. In this section we show Lemma 5.7 using the following lemma, to be
shown in Section 7.4. It essentially states the following. Assume that a height function is initially
bounded above by Hq on all of X, and by Hq′ up to an annulus Aq′
k (N Aδ) in the edge phase (see
the condition k ≥ℓ0 below), for some q′ ≤q satisfying (7.1) (which is similar to the condition
(5.14) from Lemma 5.7). Then this height function will reduce under the Glauber dynamics to
Hq′ + N δ−1, on all of X. In this way, the stronger height upper bound on inner annuli “extends”
(up to a small additive term N δ−1) to outer ones, unlike in the bulk mixing phase result Lemma 6.1,
where the weaker height upper bound “improves” on inner annuli (indeed, to show Lemma 5.7, we
will first use Lemma 5.6 to obtain a stronger height bound on the bulk liquid phase annuli, and
then “extend them out” using Lemma 7.1). See Figure 12.

The parameter d distinguishes between whether w0 is reasonably distant (d = d) or too close

8. Proof of Proposition 7.6
In this section, we prove Proposition 7.6. Recall from Section 6.3 that the proof of Proposition 6.4
was based on the notion (justified by Proposition 3.28) that the normalized limit shape p
Hq from
(3.24) is “more concave” than p
Ha,b,c.
This reasoning does not seem to directly apply to show
Proposition 7.6, since ez;q′ is not approximately constant within the cell Y−. Thus the scaling
required to define this renormalized height function p
Hq′ will vary considerably, thereby skewing the
notion of concavity, across Y−.
To remedy this, we will instead study level lines of Hq′, using Proposition 3.26 (in place of
Proposition 3.28) to realize that those of Hq′ are “more convex” than are those of Ha,b,c. We will
discuss this in more detail in the beginning of Section 8.1.2 below.
Remark 8.1. Because of the definition (7.8) of e and of the upper bound (7.7) on ω, we have
e ≤N −Bδ/2. The same considerations show that the product dw0ew0;q in (7.11) is at most N −Bδ.

8.2. Case II: d > d. In this section we establish Proposition 7.6 in the case when d > d. First
observe since d > d and since d ≤N (B+1)δ(ω/qN)1/2 ≤N (1−B/2)δ (by (7.7) and (7.8)) that we

8.2.1. Inductive setup. The proof of Proposition 7.6 if d > d will have a number of similarities
to that when d = d. However, one relevant difference relates to the estimates (3.19) and (3.22).
When d = d, we used that the former “increase in curvature” dominates the latter “error,” when
showing that y+,j
a,b,c ≤y+,j
q
(recall Definition 8.8, where the right endpoints will be the main relevant
ones when d > d) in the proof of Lemma 8.9. Now, when d > d, a direct attempt to follow the
same reasoning could have us take s0 = d (from reducing Ht(z) where dz = d) and s+ = d (from
the length of the cell Y) in Proposition 3.26. However, if d ≪d, then the error in (3.22) could
dominate the increase in curvature from (3.19), which would prevent us from ordering Hq with
respect to Ha,b,c. To circumvent this, one would like to reduce the value of s+ in Proposition 3.26,
by applying that proposition on a smaller length scale now only slightly larger than d. This would
enable us to show that y±,j
a,b,c ≤y±,j
q′ . However, if the length scale is too small, then we would not
be able to deduce that y±,j
a,b,c ≤y±,j
q
. Indeed, the difference y+,j
q
−y+,j
a,b,c would be small (as the
increase in curvature (3.19) would be used on a fairly short interval), and insufficient to overcome
the difference y+,j
q′
−y+,j
q
.
To address this, we will effectively “induct on d”; in particular, we will first show that Ht(z)
reduces when dz ∼d, and then decrement dz by factors of N δ until it is close to d. For a given
value of ¯d = dz, we will apply Proposition 3.26 with the (s0, s+) approximately equal to (¯d, N Bδ/9¯d)
here. After the first several decrements (where ¯d ≈d, so the arguments are similar to in the d = d
case), the right endpoints of these cells will by the inductive hypothesis be in regions where we have
already reduced the height of (Ht) to Hq′ (up a small additive error). In this way, we will only
have to compare Ha,b,c to Hq′ (and not to Hq), for which the smaller length scale of N Bδ/9¯d of the
interval will suffice.
The next proposition, which quickly implies Proposition 7.6, provides this inductive statement.

9. Proof of Proposition 7.7
In this section we show Proposition 7.7. At a first glance, this result may appear qualitatively
different from Proposition 7.6, since it states an improved bound (reducing the errors of NRδ to
N δ) without an improved assumption. What we will use is that the distance between the k-th
and (k + 1)-th level lines of Hq decreases as k increases (by, for example, the second part of
Corollary 3.25). So, we will choose our k here to be slightly larger (by a factor of about N8Rδ; see
(9.1)) than the value (8.5) of k in the proof of Proposition 7.6. In this way, the error of NRδ−1
in the height function around the k-th curve will correspond to a fairly small error in the vertical
(spatial) direction, of order less than d2/3N δ/2−2/3.
This error is negligible from the perspective of a point ¯z ∈X, with (d¯ze¯z;q)1/2 ≤N Rδ−1ω−1, at
which we seek to decrease (Ht) (see (9.8)). So, we will be able to vertically shift level lines under
consideration by d2/3N δ/2−2/3 (see (9.5) and Definition 9.12) while minimally affecting the height
function around ¯z, in order to account for the height difference of NRδ−1 along the k-th level line
(see Lemmas 9.3 and 9.13).
Once this shift is taken into account, the proof of Proposition 7.7 will be quite similar to that
of Proposition 7.6, and so some arguments in this section will be only outlined. In fact, several
aspects in the proof of Proposition 7.6 will simplify when showing Proposition 7.7, since the number
k of curves under consideration here will be fairly small. Indeed, we will have k ≪NCδ for some
constant C > 1, so the height function (Ht) of the Glauber dynamics below the k-th curve is at
most N Cδ−1. This directly enables us to take the essentially minimal value of Hmax ∼N Cδ−1 in
Proposition 2.13, without having to implement an auxiliary ceiling constraint (as in the pU Bernoulli
path ensemble from Definitions 8.10 and 8.24) and then showing that this constraint is irrelevant
(as in Lemmas 8.11 and 8.25).

closest to w0 is pSW

9.2. Case II: d > d. In this section we prove Proposition 7.7 when d > d. The proof will be
quite similar to that of Proposition 7.7 when d = d, and to that of Proposition 7.6 when d > d.
In fact, it will admit some simplifications with respect to the latter; specifically, since the number
of curves k ≤N 33Rδ (as noted after (9.1)) is reasonably small, we will be able to circumvent the
inductive procedure implemented there, by instead shifting the curve eU slightly to the right (see
Definition 9.12 below), in addition to down (as in (9.5)). We will also not require the point z from
Definition 8.19, as we will not need to make use of a “transition” between the two different ceiling
constraints.

Appendix A. Proof of height concentration estimate
A.1. Proof of Proposition 3.17. In this section we establish Proposition 3.17. Throughout this
section, fix real numbers R > 1 > δ > 0 and a, b, c ∈[R−1, R], as in Proposition 3.17, and further
let H denote the height function associated with a uniformly random tiling of Xa,b,c. We begin with
the following lemma, originally due to [Pet15, Lemma 5.6] (but that appears in the form stated
below as [LT15, Equation (13)]). It indicates the first statement of Proposition 3.17 holds, which
provides a concentration bound for H everywhere in Xa,b,c.
Lemma A.1 ([LT15, Equation (13)]). Item 1 of Proposition 3.17 holds.
It remains to verify the second statement of Proposition 3.17, indicating that H is w.o.p. frozen
outside of the augmented liquid region L+
a,b,c(δ).

integer i ∈[1, bN] (recall b is the vertical side-length of the hexagon) define the (random) level line

Appendix B. Properties of limit shapes
Recall from Section 3 that Hq;a,b,c denotes the limit shape for the volume-tilted random tiling
on Xa,b,c. In this section we prove the properties stated in Section 3. Throughout, we fix q ∈R
and a, b, c > 0. IWe also set q = eq and let H = {z ∈C : Im z > 0} denote the upper half plane.

xX = O(ε) (and similarly for Y ), it quickly follows for ε sufficiently small that Aq;a,b,c is a smooth,

Lemma B.9. Fix ω > 0.
For ε > 0 sufficiently small (dependent on ω), Proposition 3.27,
Lemma 3.18, and Proposition 3.28 hold (with their constant C dependent on ω) whenever ez0;q ≥ω.
Proof. Throughout this proof, we set z0 = (x0, y0). Let B = B(ω) > 1 be a constant, to be fixed
later, and let z = (x, y) ∈Lq;a,b,c be a point satisfying |z−z0| ≤B−1. Denote the (X, Y ) associated
with (x0, y0) and (x, y) by (X0, Y0) and (X, Y ), respectively.
We begin by verifying Proposition 3.27. Observe since ez0;q ≥ω ≳1, we have dz0 ≳ez0;q ≳1,
where the first statement is a consequence of Lemma 3.10 (with the fact that Aq;a,b,c is tangent to
each edge of Xa,b,c). Therefore,
dz0 ≍ez0;q ≍1.
(B.15)
By (3.24), it therefore suffices to show that |∂γHq;a,b,c(z)| ≲1 for each differential operator ∂γ
with |γ| ∈[1, B]. One way of deducing this would be through Morrey’s theorem, using the Euler–
Lagrange equation given by Lemma B.4.
However, let us do this more directly (as it will be
anyways useful for confirming the second part of the lemma), which will proceed by showing that
v is uniformly smooth in (a, b, c, x, y).
To that end, we will require several bounds on (ξ, η, ζ). Since ez0;q ≍1 (and Aq;a,b,c is defined by
the set of (x, y) for which ξ(X, Y ) = 0), it is quickly verified that −ξ(X0, Y0) ≍1; by the continuity of
ξ, it follows that −ξ(X, Y ) ≍1. One also clearly has η(X, Y ) ≲1. By (B.15), we also have b+c−x ≳
1 (where the bound follows from the fact that b + c −x is the distance from z to the northeast edge
of Xa,b,c). Therefore, for ε sufficiently small, ζ(X, Y ) = 2(A + C −X + (1 −q−1)(A + C)Y ) ≳1,
as ζ(X, Y ) ≈2 −x ≈b + c −x for ε sufficiently small; we also have that ζ(X, Y ) ≲1, since
(A, B, C, X, Y, q) are bounded. Together, these give
−ξ(X, Y ) ≍1;
η(X, Y ) ≲1;
ζ(X, Y ) ≍1.
(B.16)
By the explicit forms of (ξ, η, ζ) from (B.13), ξ, η, and ζ are all uniformly smooth (meaning that
their derivatives of order at most m are bounded by a constant depending on m) in the parameters
(A, B, C, X, Y, q), whenever |z −z0| ≤B−1. Together with (B.14) (and the fact that the map from
(A, B, C, X, Y ) to (a, b, c, x, y) is uniformly smooth for ε sufficiently small), this implies that v is
uniformly smooth in (a, b, c, x, y, q) for such z = (x, y).
We also have from (B.16) that |v| ≲1 and that Im v ≍1. These two facts, with (B.5), imply that
∂xHq;a,b,c(x, y) ≍1 and ∂yHq;a,b,c(x, y) ≍1 whenever z = (x, y) satisfies ez;q ≥ω. This confirms
the first two statements of Lemma 3.18 in this case; the third follows from integrating the first.
The proof of the fourth is entirely analogous.
Moreover, by (B.9), the map from v = v(x, y) to f = f(x, y) is (uniformly) smooth whenever
Im v ≍1 and |v| ≲1, in which case Im f ≍1 and |f| ≲1. Furthermore, we have from (B.5) that
the map from f = f(x, y) to ∇Hq;a,b,c(x, y) is (uniformly) smooth whenever Im f ≍1 and |f| ≲1.
Composing these maps, we deduce that ∇Hq;a,b,c(x, y) is (uniformly) smooth in (a, b, c, x, y, q), if
|z −z0| ≤B−1. The smoothness in (x, y) in particular verifies Proposition 3.27 whenever ez0;q ≥ω.
To confirm Proposition 3.28 in the same regime, we use Lemma B.8. Let ν = ν(ω) > 0 denote
some sufficiently small constant to be fixed later, and set

slope fx(y) for (x, y) in the liquid region but y close to γx. Underlying its proof is the fact that,

implied by the below lemma), the function f0 from (B.2) is. Thus, we will use the fact from (C.6)

C.2. Comparison between Hq at different q. In this section we show Lemma 3.19 and Lemma 3.21.
Lemma C.24. Fix real numbers q, q′, ω, κ ∈R with q′ ≤q. If Hq′(z) ≤Hq(z) −ω for all z ∈Lq
with dist(z, Aq) = κ, then Hq′(z) ≤Hq(z) −ω for all z ∈Lq with dist(z, Aq) ≥κ.
Proof. The limit shape is monotone with respect to q (by Remark 3.2) and with respect to boundary
conditions (this follows from monotonicity of the solution of the elliptic partial differential equation
(B.6) with respect to the boundary data). The lemma then follows applying these monotonicities
in the domain Λ = {z ∈Lq : dist(z, Aq) ≥κ}.
□
Fix y ∈[0, 49/100] (recall (C.1)), and let rq(y) := max{x ∈R : (x, y) ∈Aq}.
Lemma C.25. There exists a constant C > 1 such that C−1(q −q′) ≤rq(y) −rq′(y) ≤C(q −q′),
for any q, q′ ∈[−ε, ε] with q ≥q′, if ε is sufficiently small.

and (A′, B′, C′) = (−[−1]q′, [1]q′, [2]q′ −[1]q′). Also following (B.8), let X = Xq(x), X′ = Xq′(x′),
Y = Yq(y), and Y ′ = Yq′(y); further denote ∆= q −q′.
